In summary, this paper studies nonparametric DR inference methods for the derivative function of the dose-response curve with and without the positivity condition. We establish the asymptotic properties of our proposed estimators under mild conditions, permitting the use of machine learning methods for nuisance function estimation with cross-fitting. Furthermore, our identification theory and refinements of IPW and DR estimators without positivity open up a novel link between the dose-response curve inference challenge and the nonparametric set estimation problem. Simulation studies and empirical applications demonstrate the advantages of our DR estimator over the existing finite-difference method for derivative effect inference. This work also highlights several avenues for future research.

1. Bias correction for DR estimators:
As shown in
Theorem 1
and
Theorem 6
, our DR estimators of
\theta(t)
contain bias terms of order
O(h^{2})
. These biases become asymptotically negligible when the bandwidth is chosen as
h\asymp n^{-\frac{1}{5}}
that matches up the standard rate of convergence for nonparametric regression. To guarantee valid inference, an alternative approach is to explicitly estimate and correct these bias terms, as demonstrated by
Calonico et al. (2018)
;
Cheng and Chen (2019)
;
Takatsu and Westling (2025)
. A rigorous investigation of this bias-corrected approach for our DR estimators would be a valuable direction for future research.

2. Derivative estimation in other causal contexts:
Our proposed DR inference methods for
\theta(t)
can be naturally extended to conduct inference on other causal estimands of interest, such as the instantaneous causal effect
\frac{d}{dt}\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[Y(t)|\bm{S}=\bm{s}\right]

(
Stolzenberg, 1980
;
Ratkovic and Tingley, 2023
)
or the marginal direct and indirect effects in causal mediation analysis
(
Huber et al., 2020
;
Xu et al., 2021
)
.

Supplementary Materials to “Doubly Robust Inference on Causal Derivative Effects for Continuous Treatments”

Contents

Appendix A
Practical Considerations

In this section, we outline some practical aspects involved in implementing our proposed estimators of dose-response and derivative effect curves.

A.1
Self-Normalized IPW and DR Estimators

The classical IPW estimators of
m(t)
and
\theta(t)
suffer from the variance blowup when some estimated conditional densities
\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i}),i=1,...,n
are close to 0. While truncating these estimates in a threshold value can reduce the instability of IPW estimators
(
Branson et al., 2023
;
Wu et al., 2024
)
, determining the appropriate threshold value in practice is not straightforward. Alternatively, one can reduce the variances of IPW estimators and maintain its consistency by implementing the self-normalized version of IPW estimators

(
Swaminathan and Joachims, 2015
;
Kallus and Zhou, 2018
)
. This idea was originally from the importance sampling literature
(
Trotter and Tukey, 1956
)
and also known as Hájek estimator
(
Godambe and Joshi, 1965
)
.

\bullet

Estimators Under Positivity:
For the IPW estimator (
2
) of
m(t)
, its self-normalized version takes the form

\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}^{\mathrm{norm}}(t)=\frac{\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)}{\frac{1}{nh}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{j}-t}{h}\right)}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{j}|\bm{S}_{j})}}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{Y_{i}\cdot K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{j}-t}{h}\right)}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{j}|\bm{S}_{j})}}.

(29)

The self-normalized IPW estimator (
29
) maintains the consistency of the original IPW estimator (
2
), because
\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}
is a consistent estimator of
p_{T|\bm{S}}
and the (oracle) denominator of (
29
) has its expectation as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{nh}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{j}-t}{h}\right)}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{j}|\bm{S}_{j})}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h}\int_{\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{S}}\frac{K\left(\frac{t_{1}-t}{h}\right)}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t_{1}|\bm{s}_{1})}\cdot p(t_{1},\bm{s}_{1})\,d\bm{s}_{1}dt_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{S}}K(u)\cdot p_{\bm{S}}(\bm{s}_{1})\,d\bm{s}_{1}du=1

under Assumption
A5
.

Similarly, for the IPW estimator (
6
) of
\theta(t)
, its self-normalized version can be written as:

\widehat{\theta}_{\mathrm{IPW}}^{\mathrm{norm}}(t)=\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)}{\frac{1}{nh}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{j}-t}{h}\right)}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{j}|\bm{S}_{j})}}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{Y_{i}\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}}{\kappa_{2}h\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{j}-t}{h}\right)}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{j}|\bm{S}_{j})}}.

(30)

This self-normalized technique can also be applied to the IPW component of the DR estimators (
3
) and (
8
) to stabilize their variances, leading to self-normalized DR estimators of
m(t)
and
\theta(t)
as:

\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{DR}}^{\mathrm{norm}}(t)=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\left[Y_{i}-\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{i})\right]K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{j}-t}{h}\right)}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{j}|\bm{S}_{j})}}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{i})

(31)

and

\widehat{\theta}_{\mathrm{DR}}^{\mathrm{norm}}(t)=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\left[Y_{i}-\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{i})-(T_{i}-t)\cdot\widehat{\beta}(t,\bm{S}_{i})\right]\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}}{\kappa_{2}h\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{j}-t}{h}\right)}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{j}|\bm{S}_{j})}}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\beta}(t,\bm{S}_{i}),

(32)

respectively. Compared to (
9
), the estimated asymptotic variance of the self-normalized DR estimator (
32
) thus becomes

\widehat{V}_{\theta}(t)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\frac{nh\cdot\phi_{h,t}\left(Y_{i},T_{i},\bm{S}_{i};\widehat{\mu},\widehat{\beta},\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}\right)}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{j}-t}{h}\right)}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{j}|\bm{S}_{j})}}+\sqrt{h^{3}}\left[\widehat{\beta}(t,\bm{S}_{i})-\widehat{\theta}_{\mathrm{DR}}^{\mathrm{norm}}(t)\right]\right\}^{2}.

(33)

\bullet

Estimators Without Positivity:
For the bias-corrected IPW estimator (
25
) of
\theta(t)
, we also adopt the self-normalized technique to deduce that

\widehat{\theta}_{\mathrm{C,IPW}}^{\mathrm{norm}}(t)=\frac{\widehat{\theta}_{\mathrm{C,IPW}}(t)}{\frac{1}{nh}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{j}-t}{h}\right)\cdot\widehat{p}_{\zeta}(\bm{S}_{j}|t)}{\widehat{p}(T_{j},\bm{S}_{j})}}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{Y_{i}\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)\cdot\widehat{p}_{\zeta}(\bm{S}_{i}|t)}{\widehat{p}(T_{i},\bm{S}_{i})}}{\kappa_{2}h\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{j}-t}{h}\right)\cdot\widehat{p}_{\zeta}(\bm{S}_{j}|t)}{\widehat{p}(T_{j},\bm{S}_{j})}}.

(34)

The self-normalized IPW estimator (
34
) again maintains the consistency of the original IPW estimator (
25
), because
\widehat{p},\widehat{p}_{\zeta}
are consistent estimators of
p,\bar{p}_{\zeta}
respectively and the (oracle) denominator of (
34
) has its expectation as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{nh}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{j}-t}{h}\right)\cdot\bar{p}_{\zeta}(\bm{S}_{j}|t)}{p(T_{j},\bm{S}_{j})}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h}\int_{\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{S}}K\left(\frac{t_{1}-t}{h}\right)\cdot\bar{p}_{\zeta}(\bm{s}_{1}|t)\,d\bm{s}_{1}dt_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{S}}K(u)\cdot\bar{p}_{\zeta}(\bm{s}_{1}|t)\,d\bm{s}_{1}du=1

under Assumption
A5
.

Analogously, the self-normalized bias-corrected DR estimator of
\theta(t)
is given by

\widehat{\theta}_{\mathrm{C,DR}}^{\mathrm{norm}}(t)=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\left[Y_{i}-\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{i})-(T_{i}-t)\cdot\widehat{\beta}(t,\bm{S}_{i})\right]\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)\cdot\widehat{p}_{\zeta}(\bm{S}_{i}|t)}{\widehat{p}(T_{i},\bm{S}_{i})}}{\kappa_{2}h\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{j}-t}{h}\right)\cdot\widehat{p}_{\zeta}(\bm{S}_{j}|t)}{\widehat{p}(T_{j},\bm{S}_{j})}}+\int\widehat{\beta}(t,\bm{s})\cdot\widehat{p}_{\zeta}(\bm{s}|t)\,d\bm{s},

(35)

whose estimated asymptotic variance becomes

\widehat{V}_{C,\theta}(t)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\frac{nh\cdot\phi_{C,h,t}\left(Y_{i},T_{i},\bm{S}_{i};\widehat{\mu},\widehat{\beta},\widehat{p},\widehat{p}_{\zeta}\right)}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{j}-t}{h}\right)\cdot\widehat{p}_{\zeta}(\bm{S}_{j}|t)}{\widehat{p}(T_{j},\bm{S}_{j})}}+\sqrt{h^{3}}\left[\int\widehat{\beta}(t,\bm{s})\cdot\widehat{p}_{\zeta}(\bm{s}|t)\,d\bm{s}-\widehat{\theta}_{\mathrm{C,DR}}(t)\right]\right\}^{2}.

A.2
Implementation of Proposed Estimators in
Section 3
with Cross-Fitting

We explain the implementation details for our proposed estimators of
\theta(t)
in
Section 3
with cross-fitting
(
Schick, 1986
;
Newey and Robins, 2018
;
Chernozhukov et al., 2018
)
as follows. The same procedures can be applied to the estimators of
m(t)
in
Section 2.1
as well.

1. Partitioning the Data:
The observed data
\{(Y_{i},T_{i},\bm{S}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
are partitioned into
L
distinct subsets of approximately equal size. Commonly, the 5-fold (
L=5
) or 10-fold (
L=10
) cross-fitting is applied in practice, and no cross-fitting is used when
L=1
by convention. Let
I_{\ell},\ell=1,...,L
be the index sets of such a partition so that
\cup_{\ell=1}^{L}I_{\ell}=\{1,...,n\}
.

2. Estimating the Nuisance Functions:
For each index set
I_{\ell}
, we estimate the nuisance functions
\mu,\beta,p_{T|\bm{S}}
using the observations that are
not
in
I_{\ell}
; see
Section A.3
for details. The estimated nuisance functions are denoted by
\widehat{\mu}^{(\ell)},\widehat{\beta}^{(\ell)},\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{(\ell)}
, respectively, for
\ell=1,...,L
. Recall that
\mu(t,\bm{s})
is the conditional mean outcome function,
\beta(t,\bm{s})
is the partial derivative of
\mu(t,\bm{s})
with respect to
t
, and
p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s})
is the conditional density function of
T
given
\bm{S}=\bm{s}
.

3. Constructing the Final Estimators:
The RA, IPW, and DR estimators of
\theta(t)
under cross-fitting are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widehat{\theta}_{\mathrm{RA}}(t)&=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\sum_{i\in I_{\ell}}\widehat{\beta}^{(\ell)}(t,\bm{S}_{i}),\\
\widehat{\theta}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)&=\frac{1}{nh^{2}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\sum_{i\in I_{\ell}}\frac{Y_{i}\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)}{\kappa_{2}\cdot\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{(\ell)}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})},\\
\widehat{\theta}_{\mathrm{DR}}(t)&=\frac{1}{nh}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\sum_{i\in I_{\ell}}\left\{\frac{\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)}{h\cdot\kappa_{2}\cdot\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{(\ell)}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}\left[Y_{i}-\widehat{\mu}^{(\ell)}(t,\bm{S}_{i})-(T_{i}-t)\cdot\widehat{\beta}^{(\ell)}(t,\bm{S}_{i})\right]+h\cdot\widehat{\beta}^{(\ell)}(t,\bm{S}_{i})\right\},\end{split}

(36)

where the bandwidth parameter
h>0
is chosen beforehand. The estimated asymptotic variance of
\widehat{\theta}_{\mathrm{DR}}(t)
under cross-fitting is given by

\widehat{V}_{\theta}(t)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\sum_{i\in I_{\ell}}\left\{\phi_{h,t}\left(Y_{i},T_{i},\bm{S}_{i};\widehat{\mu}^{(\ell)},\widehat{\beta}^{(\ell)},\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{(\ell)}\right)+\sqrt{h^{3}}\left[\widehat{\beta}^{(\ell)}(t,\bm{S}_{i})-\widehat{\theta}_{\mathrm{DR}}(t)\right]\right\}^{2}.

(37)

The self-normalized technique in
Section A.1
can be applied to these cross-fitted estimators accordingly.

A.3
Nuisance Function Estimation

The implementation of the DR estimator (
8
) of
\theta(t)
in
Section 3
requires the estimation of three nuisance functions: (i) the conditional mean outcome function
\mu(t,\bm{s})=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left(Y|T=t,\bm{S}=\bm{s}\right)
; (ii) the partial derivative function
\beta(t,\bm{s})=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s})
; and (iii) the conditional density
p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s})
. Below, we discuss how these nuisance functions are estimated in our numerical experiments of
Section 6
.

\bullet

Estimations of
\mu(t,\bm{s})
and
\beta(t,\bm{s})
:
We apply a fully connected neural network model with two hidden layers of size
100\times 50
and use the sigmoid linear unit function
u\mapsto\frac{u}{1+e^{-u}}
as the activation function to ensure the smoothness of resulting estimators
\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{s})
and
\widehat{\beta}(t,\bm{s})
. Other choices of the neural network architectures and activation functions also works for our proposed DR estimator (
8
). Theoretically, Theorem 1 in
Farrell et al. (2021)
and Section 3.1 in
Colangelo and Lee (2020)
discuss some regularity conditions under which our requirements on the rates of convergence are satisfied by neural network models. Practically, our neural network model is implemented via
PyTorch

(
Paszke et al., 2019
)
, and we use its automatic differentiation engine
(
Paszke et al., 2017
)
to compute the estimated partial derivative
\widehat{\beta}(t,\bm{s})
from the fitted conditional mean outcome function
\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{s})
. In contrast to numerical differentiation, automatic differentiation offers the key advantage of being hyperparameter-free and inherently accurate to working precision
(
Baydin et al., 2018
;
Blondel and Roulet, 2024
)
. Recently, it has been employed to compute the semi-parametric or non-parametric efficient influence function for any statistical functional
(
Luedtke, 2024
)
.

\bullet

Estimation of
p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s})
:
Given the data-generating model (
27
), we consider two different methods for estimating the conditional density
p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s})
with kernel smoothing techniques.

1.

Method 1 (Kernel density estimation (KDE) on residuals):
Notice that the relationship between the covariate vector
\bm{S}
and the treatment variation variable
E
is additive in model (
27
),
i.e.
,

T=g_{\bm{S}}(\bm{S})+g_{E}(E)\quad\text{ with }\quad g_{\bm{S}}(\bm{S})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{3\bm{\xi}^{T}\bm{S}}\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{2}\right)du-0.5\;\text{ and }\;g_{E}(E)=0.75E.

In addition, the regression function of
T
against
\bm{S}
is given by
\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left(T|\bm{S}=\bm{s}\right)=g_{\bm{S}}(\bm{s})
. To estimate the regression function
g_{\bm{S}}
, we can apply any machine learning method to the data
\{(T_{i},\bm{S}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
using cross-fitting. In the actual implementation, we use a neural network model with one hidden layer of size 20 and rectified linear unit function
u\mapsto\max\{0,u\}
as the activation function. Based on the fitted regression function
\widehat{g}_{\bm{S}}
, we construct an estimator of
p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s})
as:

\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s})=\frac{1}{nh_{e}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K_{e}\left[\frac{t-\widehat{g}_{\bm{S}}(\bm{s})-\left(T_{i}-\widehat{g}_{\bm{S}}(\bm{S}_{i})\right)}{h_{e}}\right],

where the kernel function
K_{e}
and bandwidth
h_{e}>0
may differ from those in our DR estimator (
8
). It is worth noting that the observations
\{T_{1}-\widehat{g}_{\bm{S}}(\bm{S}_{1}),...,T_{n}-\widehat{g}_{\bm{S}}(\bm{S}_{n})\}
are not i.i.d., necessitating additional analysis for asymptotic theory and bandwidth selection
(
Hart and Vieu, 1990
;
Meloche, 1990
)
. For simplicity, we use the Epanechnikov kernel
K_{e}(u)=\frac{3}{4}(1-|u|)\,\mathbbm{1}_{\{|u|\leq 1\}}
and choose the bandwidth via Silverman’s rule of thumb as
\widehat{h}_{e}=\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)^{\frac{1}{5}}\widehat{\sigma}_{e}n^{-\frac{1}{5}}
, where
\widehat{\sigma}_{e}
is the sample standard deviation of
\{T_{1}-\widehat{g}_{\bm{S}}(\bm{S}_{1}),...,T_{n}-\widehat{g}_{\bm{S}}(\bm{S}_{n})\}
.

2.

Method 2 (Regression on kernel-smoothed outcomes (RKS))
The validity of Method 1 relies on the additive relation between
\bm{S}
and
E
in the model for
T
. Since this additive structure may not hold in general, we consider another kernel smoothing method for estimating
p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s})
. Specifically, we estimate a kernel-smoothed regression function
g(t,\bm{s})=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[K_{r}\left(\frac{T-t}{h_{r}}\right)\big|\bm{S}=\bm{s}\right]
by regressing kernel-smoothed outcomes
\left\{K_{r}\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h_{r}}\right)\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
against the covariate vectors
\left\{\bm{S}_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{n}
via any machine learning method. The fitted kernel-smoothed regression function
\widehat{g}(t,\bm{s})
is a consistent estimator of
p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s})
when the regression method is accurate, because

\displaystyle g(t,\bm{s})

\displaystyle=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[K_{r}\left(\frac{T-t}{h_{r}}\right)\Big|\bm{S}=\bm{s}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{T}}K_{r}\left(\frac{t_{1}-t}{h_{r}}\right)p_{T|\bm{S}}(t_{1}|\bm{s})\,dt_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{r}(u)\cdot p_{T|\bm{S}}(t+uh_{r}|\bm{s})\,du

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{r}(u)\left[p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s})+uh_{r}\cdot p_{T|\bm{S}}^{\prime}(t|\bm{s})+\frac{u^{2}h_{r}^{2}}{2}\cdot p_{T|\bm{S}}^{\prime\prime}(t|\bm{s})+o\left(h_{r}^{3}\right)\right]\,du

\displaystyle=p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s})+O\left(h_{r}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\to p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s})

as
h_{r}\to 0
under Assumptions
A4
and
A5
(a-b). In the actual implementation, we again use a neural network model with one hidden layer of size 20 and rectified linear unit function
u\mapsto\max\{0,u\}
as the activation function.
Here, the kernel function
K_{r}
and bandwidth
h_{r}>0
can be different from those in our DR estimator (
8
). To ensure a relatively large effective sample size for fitting
g
, we use the Gaussian kernel
K_{r}(u)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{2}\right)
and choose the bandwidth by Silverman’s rule of thumb as
\widehat{h}_{r}=\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)^{\frac{1}{5}}\widehat{\sigma}_{T}n^{-\frac{1}{5}}
, where
\widehat{\sigma}_{T}
is the sample standard deviation of
\{T_{1},...,T_{n}\}
.

Besides that,
Klosin (2021)
proposed another method with kernel smoothing that directly estimates the reciprocal
\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s})}
of the conditional density using a minimum distance Lasso approach
(
Chernozhukov et al., 2022
)
. This method employs polynomial basis functions of the covariate vector
\bm{S}
and a kernel-smoothed
L_{2}
loss function. We briefly experimented with this approach and found that its performance and computational efficiency are inferior to the two methods above. In addition, this approach is very sensitive to the choice of its tuning parameter as shown in Section 6 of
Klosin (2021)
. Thus, we choose not to report its results.

Appendix B
Additional Simulation Results

This section provides supplementary simulation results assessing the impact of varying the bandwidth parameter on the performance of our proposed estimators of
\theta(t)
and the finite-difference method by
Colangelo and Lee (2020)
. Furthermore, we evaluate the finite-sample performances of our proposed estimators without cross-fitting in both simulation studies and the empirical analysis of the U.S. Job Corps Program dataset.

B.1
Simulation Studies With Positivity Across Different Bandwidth Choices

We follow the same data-generating process and experimental setup in
Section 6.1
to evaluate the performances of different estimators of
\theta(t)
under the positivity condition, varying the bandwidth parameter
h
. In line with the bandwidth choices in
Colangelo and Lee (2020)
;
Klosin (2021)
, we examine four scaling factors for the bandwidth parameter as
h=C_{h}\cdot\widehat{\sigma}_{T}\cdot n^{-\frac{1}{5}}
with
C_{T}\in\left\{0.75,1,1.25,1.5\right\}
, where
\widehat{\sigma}_{T}
is the sample standard deviation of
\{T_{1},...,T_{n}\}
. For supplementary purposes, we only present the simulation results with 5-fold cross-fitting when the sample size is
n=4000
in
Figure 6
. The results are mostly consistent with our findings in
Section 6.1
.
Our proposed DR estimators, leveraging either true or KDE-estimated conditional densities, demonstrate lower estimation biases and superior empirical coverage probabilities for their confidence intervals compared to the finite-difference method of
Colangelo and Lee (2020)
. At the same time, they maintain RMSEs that are comparable to the finite-difference method. This additional results further demonstrate the robustness of our proposed DR estimator (
8
) to variations in its bandwidth parameter.

Figure 6
:
Comparisons between our proposed estimators and the finite-difference approaches by
Colangelo and Lee (2020)
(“CL20”) under positivity and with 5-fold cross-fitting across different bandwidth values (
h
). Rows present estimation bias, RMSE, and coverage probability for each estimator of
\theta(t)
, while columns correspond to different scaling factors for
h
.

B.2
Simulation Studies With Positivity and No Cross-Fitting

For exploratory purposes, we conduct additional simulations to compare the performances of our proposed estimators of
\theta(t)
in
Section 3
with the finite-difference method by
Colangelo and Lee (2020)
when cross-fitting is not employed.

We replicate the experimental setup in
Section 6.1
to generate the simulation results shown in
Figure 7
across various sample sizes without using any cross-fitting. When the sample size is small, our proposed DR estimators without cross-fitting exhibit lower RMSEs but higher estimation biases than the finite-difference method by
Colangelo and Lee (2020)
, resulting in inferior empirical coverage probabilities for the associated confidence intervals. However, as the sample size increases, the estimation biases of our DR estimators diminish, and the empirical coverage probabilities of their confidence intervals improve, ultimately surpassing the finite-sample performance of the finite-difference method by
Colangelo and Lee (2020)
.

Figure 7
:
Comparisons between our proposed estimators and the finite-difference approaches by
Colangelo and Lee (2020)
(“CL20”) under positivity and without cross-fitting across various sample sizes. Rows present estimation bias, RMSE, and coverage probability for each estimator of
\theta(t)
, while columns correspond to different values for
n
. This figure follows an identical simulation setup as
Figure 3
but without using any cross-fitting.

These results without cross-fitting again highlight the practical utility of our proposed estimators in
Section 3
under the positivity condition. However, developing rigorous theoretical guarantees for these estimators without cross-fitting is beyond the scope of this paper and will be addressed in future work.

B.3
Simulation Studies Without Positivity Across Different Bandwidth Choices

We adopt the same data-generating process and experimental setup in
Section 6.2
to evaluate the performances of different estimators of
\theta(t)
under various choices of the bandwidth parameter without assuming the positivity condition. Specifically, we test four scaling factors for the bandwidth parameter as
h=C_{h}\cdot\widehat{\sigma}_{T}\cdot n^{-\frac{1}{5}}
with
C_{T}\in\left\{0.75,1,1.5,2\right\}
, where
\widehat{\sigma}_{T}
is the sample standard deviation of
\{T_{1},...,T_{n}\}
. For supplementary purposes, we only present the simulation results with 5-fold cross-fitting when the sample size is
n=2000
in
Figure 8
. Again, our proposed bias-corrected estimators of
\theta(t)
demonstrate significant improvements by reducing bias and enhancing the empirical coverage probabilities of the resulting confidence intervals compared to their counterparts in
Section 3
across various bandwidth parameter choices.

Figure 8
:
Comparisons between our bias-corrected estimators (NP) in
Section 5.1
and their counterparts (P) under the violation of positivity and with 5-fold cross-fitting (
L=5
) across different bandwidth values (
h
). Rows present estimation bias, RMSE, and coverage probability for each estimator of
\theta(t)
, while columns correspond to different scaling factors for
h
.

B.4
Simulation Studies Without Positivity and No Cross-Fitting

For exploratory purposes, we conduct additional simulations for our bias-corrected estimators of
\theta(t)
in
Section 5.1
when the positivity condition is violated and cross-fitting is not employed.

Using the same experimental setup described in
Section 6.2
, we generate simulation results, shown in
Figure 9
, across various sample sizes without using any cross-fitting. As expected, the estimation biases and RMSEs of our bias-corrected estimators improve as the sample size increases, consistently outperforming their standard counterparts. However, in comparison to the results obtained with 5-fold cross-fitting in
Figure 4
, the performance of our bias-corrected estimators without cross-fitting deteriorates, particularly in terms of the empirical coverage probabilities of the resulting confidence intervals. These results consolidate the need of cross-fitting for constructing our bias-corrected estimators as
Theorem 6
suggests.

:
Comparisons between our bias-corrected estimators (NP) in
Section 5.1
and their counterparts (P) in
Section 3
under the violation of positivity and without cross-fitting across different sample sizes. Rows present estimation bias, RMSE, and coverage probability for each estimator of
\theta(t)
, while columns correspond to different values for
n
. This figure follows an identical simulation setup as in
Figure 4
but without using any cross-fitting.

B.5
Analysis of the Job Corps Program With No Cross-fitted Estimators

Finally, we explore the behaviors of our proposed DR estimator (
8
) and the finite-difference method by
Colangelo and Lee (2020)
when cross-fitting is not employed. Following the same analysis pipeline described in
Section 6.3
, but without employing cross-fitting, we produce the results shown in
Figure 10
. Interestingly, our DR estimator without cross-fitting reveals some distinct trends compared to its counterpart with 5-fold cross-fitting. Specifically, it suggests a positive impact on employment during the first 20 weeks (
\sim 800
hours), diminishing benefits after 23 weeks (
\sim 920
hours), and statistically significant negative effects beyond 43 weeks (
\sim 1720
hours). These trends align with prior research (
e.g.
, Figure 2 of
Lee 2009
), which documented short-term negative impacts of the program on employment propensities (104 weeks after the program assignment). However, it is worth mentioning that the analysis in
Lee (2009)
was based on a binary treatment variable of being in the program or not. Since we do not establish any theoretical guarantees for our DR estimator of
\theta(t)
when cross-fitting is not applied in this paper, more thorough investigations are necessary in the future to substantiate these short-term negative impacts of the Job Corps program.

Figure 10
:
Estimated derivative effect curves with 95% confidence intervals using our proposed estimators and the finite-difference approaches by
Colangelo and Lee (2020)
(“CL20”) without cross-fitting. The vertical red dotted lines mark the original treatment range
[320,1840]
analyzed in
Colangelo and Lee (2020)
. This figure follows an identical analysis pipeline as in
Figure 5
but without using any cross-fitting.

Appendix C
Identification of
m(t)
and
\theta(t)
Under the Additive Confounding Model (
13
)

Proposition C.1

(Identifications of
m(t)
and
\theta(t)
)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
A1
(a-c) and
A4
(c) holds under model (
13
). Then, for any
t\in\mathcal{T}
with
p_{\bm{S}|T}(\bm{s}|t)>0
for some
\bm{s}\in\mathcal{S}
, we have that

\theta(t)=\bar{m}^{\prime}(t)=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(T,\bm{S})\Big|T=t\right],

where
\mu(t,\bm{s})=\mathbb{E}(Y|T=t,\bm{S}=\bm{s})
. If, in addition, the marginal support
\mathcal{T}
of
p_{T}(t)
is connected, then

m(t)=\mathbb{E}\left[Y+\int_{T}^{t}\theta(\widetilde{t})\,d\widetilde{t}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left\{Y+\int_{T}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(T,\bm{S})\Big|T=\widetilde{t}\right]\,d\widetilde{t}\right\}.

Proof of Proposition
C.1
.

We first study the identification of
\theta(t)
. By (
14
),
\theta(t)=\bar{m}^{\prime}(t)
, and the conditional mean outcome function
\mu(t,\bm{s})=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left(Y|T=t,\bm{S}=\bm{s}\right)=\bar{m}(t)+\eta(\bm{s})
is well-defined within the support
\mathcal{J}
of the joint density
p(t,\bm{s})
. In particular, for any
t\in\mathcal{T}
with
p_{\bm{S}|T}(\widetilde{\bm{s}}|t)>0
for some
\widetilde{\bm{s}}\in\mathcal{S}
, we know that
p(t,\widetilde{\bm{s}})>0
so that
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\widetilde{\bm{s}})=\bar{m}^{\prime}(t)
is also well-defined for these
\widetilde{\bm{s}}\in\mathcal{S}(t)
. Furthermore, under Assumption
A4
(c), the support
\mathcal{S}(t)
of the conditional distribution
p_{\bm{S}|T}(\bm{s}|t)
is non-degenerate (
i.e.
, has nonzero Lebesgue measure). Thus,

\theta(t)=\bar{m}^{\prime}(t)=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(T,\bm{S})\Big|T=t\right]

is valid.

As for the identification of
m(t)
, we apply the fundamental theorem of calculus and argue that

m(t)=m(T)+\int_{T}^{t}\theta(\widetilde{t})\,d\widetilde{t}.

Taking the expectation over
T
yields that

m(t)=\mathbb{E}\left[m(T)+\int_{T}^{t}\theta(\widetilde{t})\,d\widetilde{t}\right]=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left\{Y+\int_{T}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(T,\bm{S})\Big|T=\widetilde{t}\right]\,d\widetilde{t}\right\},

where the second equality follows from the fact that
\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[m(T)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{m}(T)\right]+\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[\eta(\bm{S})\right]=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}(Y)
by (
14
). Here, the connectedness of
\mathcal{T}
ensures that the integration of
\theta(t)
is only over the region where it is identifiable. When
\mathcal{T}
has multiple connected components, the integral formula (
15
) as well as the observations should be restricted to the connected component in which the point of interest
t\in\mathcal{T}
lies.
∎

Appendix D
Asymptotic Differences Between Two Variants of IPW Estimators

In this section, we study the asymptotic differences between the IPW estimators when the inverse probability weights are evaluated at the sample points
(T_{i},\bm{S}_{i}),i=1,...,n
or at the (query) points
(t,\bm{S}_{i}),i=1,...,n
. Specifically, for estimating the dose-response curve
m(t)
, we have two variants of the IPW estimators as (
2
) and (
4
). Similarly, for estimating the derivative effect
\theta(t)=m^{\prime}(t)
, we also consider two different versions of the IPW estimators as (
6
) and (
7
). For the sake of illustrations, we assume that the conditional density
p_{T|\bm{S}}
is known and only consider the oracle IPW estimators.

D.1
Asymptotic Difference Between IPW Estimators (
2
) and (
4
) of
m(t)

We define the difference between two oracle IPW estimators of
m(t)
as:

\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,m}}(t)=\widetilde{m}_{\mathrm{IPW,2}}(t)-\widetilde{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)=\frac{1}{nh}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S}_{i})}-\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}\right]Y_{i}\cdot K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right).

(38)

Proposition D.1
.

Suppose that Assumptions
A2
,
A3
,
A4
, and
A5
hold with
\mu(t,\bm{s})=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}(Y|T=t,\bm{S}=\bm{s})
. Then, for any fixed
t\in\mathcal{T}
, we have that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,m}}(t)

\displaystyle=h^{2}\kappa_{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}+\frac{\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}{2p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}\right]+O(h^{3})+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{h}{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=O(h^{2})+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{h}{n}}\right)

as
h\to 0
and
n\to\infty
.

Proof of Proposition
D.1
.

By Chebyshev’s inequality, we know that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,m}}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,m}}(t)\right]+\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,m}}(t)-\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,m}}(t)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,m}}(t)\right]+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,m}}(t)\right]}\right).

On one hand, we calculate that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,m}}(t)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\{\frac{1}{h}\left[\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}-\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\right]Y\cdot K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\{\int_{\mathcal{T}}\left[\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}-\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t_{1}|\bm{S})}\right]\mu(t_{1},\bm{S})\cdot K\left(\frac{t_{1}-t}{h}\right)\cdot p_{T|\bm{S}}(t_{1}|\bm{S})\,dt_{1}\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\frac{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t+uh|\bm{S})}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}-1\right]\mu(t+uh,\bm{S})\cdot K(u)\,du\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(ii)}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\frac{uh\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})+\frac{u^{2}h^{2}}{2}\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}+O(h^{3})\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\times\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})+uh\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})+\frac{u^{2}h^{2}}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mu(t,\bm{S})+O(h^{3})\right]K(u)\,du\bigg\}

\displaystyle=h^{2}\kappa_{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}+\frac{\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}{2p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}\right]+O(h^{3})

where (i) uses a change of variable
u=\frac{t_{1}-t}{h}
while (ii) applies Taylor’s expansions on
p_{T|\bm{S}}
and
\mu
under Assumptions
A3
and
A4
. On the other hand, we also compute that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,m}}(t)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nh^{2}}\cdot\mathrm{Var}\left\{\left[\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}-\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\right]Y\cdot K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(iii)}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{nh^{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left\{\left[\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}-\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\right]^{2}Y^{2}K^{2}\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)\right\}+O\left(\frac{h^{4}}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(iv)}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{nh}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left\{\left[\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}-\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t+uh|\bm{S})}\right]^{2}\left[\mu(t+uh,\bm{S})^{2}+\sigma^{2}\right]K^{2}(u)\cdot p_{T|\bm{S}}(t+uh|\bm{S})\,du\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(v)}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{nh}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left\{\frac{\left[uh\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})+\frac{u^{2}h^{2}}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})+O(h^{3})\right]^{2}}{p_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(t|\bm{S})\cdot p_{T|\bm{S}}(t+uh|\bm{S})}\cdot\left[\mu(t+uh,\bm{S})^{2}+\sigma^{2}\right]K^{2}(u)\,du\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{h}{n}\cdot\nu_{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left\{\frac{\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\right]^{2}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})^{2}+\sigma^{2}\right]}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}\right\}+O\left(\frac{h^{2}}{n}\right)

where (iii) leverages our above calculation on
\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,m}}(t)\right]=O(h^{2})
, (iv) applies a change of variable, and (v) utilizes Taylor’s expansion on
p_{T|\bm{S}}
under Assumption
A4
.

In total, we conclude that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,m}}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,m}}(t)\right]+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,m}}(t)\right]}\right)

\displaystyle=h^{2}\kappa_{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}+\frac{\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}{2p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}\right]+O(h^{3})+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{h}{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=O(h^{2})+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{h}{n}}\right)

as
h\to 0
and
n\to\infty
. The result follows.
∎

D.2
Asymptotic Difference Between IPW Estimators (
6
) and (
7
) of
\theta(t)

We define the difference between two oracle IPW estimators of
\theta(t)
as:

\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,\theta}}(t)=\widetilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{IPW,2}}(t)-\widetilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)=\frac{1}{nh}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S}_{i})}-\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}\right]\frac{Y_{i}\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h^{2}}\right)K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)}{\kappa_{2}}.

(39)

Proposition D.2
.

Suppose that Assumptions
A2
,
A3
,
A4
, and
A5
hold with
\mu(t,\bm{s})=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}(Y|T=t,\bm{S}=\bm{s})
. Then, for any fixed
t\in\mathcal{T}
, we have that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,\theta}}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{h^{2}\kappa_{4}}{2\kappa_{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})+\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}+\frac{\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial^{3}}{\partial t^{3}}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}{3p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+O(h^{3})+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{nh}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\right]+O(h^{2})+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{nh}}\right)

as
h\to 0
and
nh\to\infty
.

Proof of Proposition
D.2
.

By Chebyshev’s inequality, we know that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,\theta}}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,\theta}}(t)\right]+\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,\theta}}(t)-\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,\theta}}(t)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,\theta}}(t)\right]+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,\theta}}(t)\right]}\right).

On one hand, we calculate that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,\theta}}(t)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\{\left[\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}-\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\right]\frac{Y\left(\frac{T-t}{h^{2}}\right)K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{h\cdot\kappa_{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\{\int_{\mathcal{T}}\left[\frac{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t_{1}|\bm{S})}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}-1\right]\frac{\mu(t_{1},\bm{S})\left(\frac{t_{1}-t}{h^{2}}\right)K\left(\frac{t_{1}-t}{h}\right)}{h\cdot\kappa_{2}}\,dt_{1}\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\frac{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t+uh|\bm{S})}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}-1\right]\frac{\mu(t+uh,\bm{S})\cdot u\cdot K(u)}{h\cdot\kappa_{2}}\,du\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(ii)}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\frac{uh\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})+\frac{u^{2}h^{2}}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})+\frac{u^{3}h^{3}}{6}\frac{\partial^{3}}{\partial t^{3}}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})+O(h^{4})}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\times\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})+uh\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})+\frac{u^{2}h^{2}}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mu(t,\bm{S})+O(h^{3})\right]\frac{u\cdot K(u)}{h\cdot\kappa_{2}}\,du\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{h^{2}\kappa_{4}}{2\kappa_{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})+\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}+\frac{\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial^{3}}{\partial t^{3}}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}{3p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}\right]+O(h^{3}),

where (i) uses a change of variable
u=\frac{t_{1}-t}{h}
while (ii) applies Taylor’s expansions on
p_{T|\bm{S}}
and
\mu
under Assumptions
A3
and
A4
. On the other hand, we also compute that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,\theta}}(t)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nh^{2}}\cdot\mathrm{Var}\left\{\left[\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}-\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\right]\frac{Y\left(\frac{T-t}{h^{2}}\right)K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{\kappa_{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nh^{4}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left\{\left[\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}-\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\right]^{2}\frac{Y^{2}\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)^{2}K^{2}\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{\kappa_{2}^{2}}\right\}+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(iii)}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{nh^{4}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left\{\left[\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}-\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\right]^{2}\frac{\left[\mu(T,\bm{S})^{2}+\sigma^{2}\right]\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)^{2}K^{2}\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{\kappa_{2}^{2}}\right\}+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(iv)}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{nh^{3}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left[\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}-\frac{1}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t+uh|\bm{S})}\right]^{2}\frac{\left[\mu(t+uh,\bm{S})^{2}+\sigma^{2}\right]u^{2}K^{2}(u)}{\kappa_{2}^{2}}\cdot p_{T|\bm{S}}(t+uh|\bm{S})\,du\right\}+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(v)}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{nh^{3}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigg\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\left[uh\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})+\frac{u^{2}h^{2}}{2}\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})+\frac{u^{3}h^{3}}{6}\cdot\frac{\partial^{3}}{\partial t^{3}}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})+O(h^{4})\right]^{2}}{p_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(t|\bm{S})\cdot p_{T|\bm{S}}(t+uh|\bm{S})}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\times\frac{\left[\mu(t+uh,\bm{S})^{2}+\sigma^{2}\right]u^{2}K^{2}(u)}{\kappa_{2}^{2}}\,du\bigg\}+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu_{4}}{nh\cdot\kappa_{2}^{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left\{\frac{\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\right]^{2}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})^{2}+\sigma^{2}\right]}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}\right\}+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right),

where (iii) leverages our above calculation on
\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,\theta}}(t)\right]=O(1)
, (iv) applies a change of variable, and (v) utilizes Taylor’s expansion on
p_{T|\bm{S}}
under Assumption
A4
.

In total, we conclude that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,\theta}}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,\theta}}(t)\right]+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}\left[\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,\theta}}(t)\right]}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{h^{2}\kappa_{4}}{2\kappa_{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})+\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}+\frac{\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial^{3}}{\partial t^{3}}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}{3p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+O(h^{3})+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{nh}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\right]+O(h^{2})+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{nh}}\right)

as
h\to 0
and
nh\to\infty
. The result follows.
∎

Remark D.1
.

Given our convergence analysis for
\widehat{\theta}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)
in
Theorem 1
, one can easily calculate that the non-vanishing bias term
\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\right]
for
\widetilde{\Delta}_{\mathrm{IPW,\theta}}(t)
in Proposition
D.2
results from the IPW estimator
\widetilde{\theta}_{\mathrm{IPW,2}}(t)
in (
7
). Therefore, unless
\mu(t,\bm{s})=0
or
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s})=0
, the IPW estimator (
7
) of
\theta(t)
is asymptotically biased and inconsistent.

Appendix E
Consistency of Estimating
m(t)
Under Positivity

In this section, we review and prove the consistency results of
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{RA}}(t)
,
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)
, and
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{DR}}(t)
in (
1
), (
2
), (
3
) for estimating the dose-response curve
t\mapsto m(t)=\mbox{$\mathbb{E}$}\left[Y(t)\right]
under the positivity condition.

Proposition E.1

(Consistency of Estimating
m(t)
Under Positivity)
.

Suppose that Assumptions
A1
,
A3
,
A4
,
A5
, and
A2
hold as well as
\widehat{\mu},\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}
are constructed on a data sample independent of
\{(Y_{i},T_{i},\bm{S}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
. For any fixed
t\in\mathcal{T}
, we let
\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{s})
and
\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s})
be fixed bounded functions to which
\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{s})
and
\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s})
converge under the rates of convergence as:

\left|\left|\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}=O_{P}\left(\Upsilon_{1,n}\right)\quad\text{ and }\quad\sup_{|u-t|\leq h}\left|\left|\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})-\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}=O_{P}\left(\Upsilon_{2,n}\right),

where
\Upsilon_{1,n},\Upsilon_{2,n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Then, as
h\to 0
and
nh\to\infty
, we have that

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{RA}}(t)-m(t)=O_{P}\left(\Upsilon_{1,n}+\left|\left|\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\mu(t,\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right),

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)-m(t)=O(h^{2})+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{nh}}+\Upsilon_{2,n}+\sup\limits_{|u-t|\leq h}\left|\left|\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})-p_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}\right).

If, in addition, we assume that

(a)

\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}
satisfies Assumptions
A4
and
A2
;

(b)

either (i) “
\,\bar{\mu}=\mu
” or “
\,\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}=p_{T|\bm{S}}
” almost surely;

(c)

\sqrt{nh}\left|\left|\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\mu(t,\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}\sup_{|u-t|\leq h}\left|\left|\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})-p_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}=o_{P}(1)
,

then

\sqrt{nh}\left[\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{DR}}(t)-m(t)\right]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\psi_{h,t}\left(Y_{i},T_{i},\bm{S}_{i};\bar{\mu},\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}\right)+\sqrt{h}\left[\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{i})-\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]\right]\right\}+o_{P}(1)

when
nh^{5}\to c_{2}
for some finite number
c_{2}\geq 0
, where
\psi_{h,t}\left(Y,T,\bm{S};\bar{\mu},\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}\right)=\frac{K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{\sqrt{h}\cdot\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\left[Y-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]
and

\sqrt{nh}\left[\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{DR}}(t)-m(t)-h^{2}B_{m}(t)\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}\left(0,V_{m}(t)\right)

with
V_{m}(t)=\mathbb{E}\left[\psi_{h,t}^{2}\left(Y,T,\bm{S};\bar{\mu},\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}\right)\right]
and

\displaystyle B_{m}(t)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{\kappa_{2}}{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\bm{S}}\left\{\frac{2\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})+p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\left[\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mu(t,\bm{S})-2\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}\right\}\quad\text{ when }\bar{\mu}=\mu,\\
\frac{\kappa_{2}}{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\bm{S}}\left[\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]\quad\quad\text{ when }\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}=p_{T|\bm{S}}.\end{cases}

Remark E.1

(Uniform asymptotic theory for estimating
m(t)
)
.

If we assume that

\begin{cases}\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\left|\left|\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}=O_{P}(\Upsilon_{1,n}),\\
\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\left|\left|\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})-\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}=O_{P}\left(\Upsilon_{2,n}\right),\\
\sqrt{nh}\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\left|\left|\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\mu(t,\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}\left|\left|\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})-p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}=o_{P}(1),\end{cases}

then the pointwise convergence results in Proposition
E.1
can be strengthened to the uniform ones; see our side notes in the proof below.

Proof of Proposition
E.1
.

We derive the rates of convergence of
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{RA}}(t)
given by (
1
) and
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)
given by (
2
) in
Section E.1
and
Section E.2
, respectively. We also prove the asymptotic linearity, double robustness, and asymptotic normality of
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{DR}}(t)
given by (
3
) in
Section E.3
.

E.1
Rate of Convergence of
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{RA}}(t)

Firstly, we derive the rate of convergence for
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{RA}}(t)
in (
1
). Notice that

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{RA}}(t)-m(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{i})-\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S}_{1})\right]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{i})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{i})\right]}_{\textbf{Term I}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{i})-\mu(t,\bm{S}_{i})\right]}_{\textbf{Term II}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\mu(t,\bm{S}_{i})-\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S}_{i})\right]\right\}}_{\textbf{Term III}}.

\bullet

Term I:
By Markov’s inequality (and Hölder’s inequality), we know that

Term I

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{i})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{i})\right|

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\mathbb{E}\left|\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{1})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{1})\right|\right)=O_{P}\left(\left|\left|\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}\right)=O_{P}\left(\Upsilon_{1,n}\right).

\bullet

Term II:
We similarly derive that

Term II

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{i})-\mu(t,\bm{S}_{i})\right|=O_{P}\left(\left|\left|\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\mu(t,\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}\right).

\bullet

Term III:
By the central limit theorem and the boundedness of
\mu(t,\bm{s})
on
\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{S}
under Assumption
A3
, we know that

\textbf{Term III}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\mu(t,\bm{S}_{i})-\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S}_{i})\right]\right\}=O_{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right).

As a side note, under Assumption
A3
, we know that
\left|\mu(t_{1},\bm{s})-\mu(t_{2},\bm{s})\right|\leq A_{1}|t_{1}-t_{2}|
for some absolute constant
A_{1}>0
. Together with the compactness of
\mathcal{T}
and Example 19.7 in
van der Vaart (1998)
, we also deduce that

\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\mu(t,\bm{S}_{i})-\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S}_{i})\right]\right\}\right|=O_{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right).

In summary, we conclude that

\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{RA}}(t)-m(t)=O_{P}\left(\Upsilon_{1,n}+\left|\left|\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\mu(t,\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right).

E.2
Rate of Convergence of
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)

Secondly, we derive the rate of convergence for
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)
in (
2
). Note that

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)-m(t)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)-m(t)+\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)-\widetilde{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{1}{nh}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}\cdot Y_{i}-\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S}_{1})\right]}_{\textbf{Term IV}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{nh}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}\cdot Y_{i}-\frac{1}{nh}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}\cdot Y_{i}}_{\textbf{Term V}},

where
\widetilde{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)=\frac{1}{nh}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}Y_{i}
is the oracle IPW estimator of
m(t)
defined in (
18
). We shall handle
Term IV
and
Term V
in
Section E.2.1
and
Section E.2.2
, respectively.

E.2.1
Rate of Convergence of
Term IV
for
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)

Under Assumptions
A3
and
A5
, we calculate the bias of
\widetilde{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)
as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)\right]-m(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{h}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}\cdot Y_{i}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S}_{1})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h}\int_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{T}}\frac{K\left(\frac{t_{1}-t}{h}\right)}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t_{1}|\bm{s}_{1})}\cdot\mu(t_{1},\bm{s}_{1})\cdot p(t_{1},\bm{s}_{1})\,dt_{1}d\bm{s}_{1}-\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S}_{1})\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{=}}\int_{\mathcal{S}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}K(u)\cdot\mu(t+uh,\bm{s}_{1})\cdot p_{S}(\bm{s}_{1})\,dud\bm{s}_{1}-\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S}_{1})\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(ii)}}}{{=}}\int_{\mathcal{S}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}K(u)\left[\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})+uh\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})+\frac{u^{2}h^{2}}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mu(\widetilde{t},\bm{s}_{1})\right]p_{S}(\bm{s}_{1})\,dud\bm{s}_{1}-\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S}_{1})\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(iii)}}}{{=}}\int_{\mathcal{S}}\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})\cdot p_{S}(\bm{s}_{1})\,d\bm{s}_{1}-\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S}_{1})\right]+\int_{\mathcal{S}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}K(u)\cdot\frac{u^{2}h^{2}}{2}\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mu(\widetilde{t},\bm{s}_{1})\cdot p_{S}(\bm{s}_{1})\,dud\bm{s}_{1}

\displaystyle=O(h^{2}),

where (i) uses a change of variable
u=\frac{t_{1}-t}{h}
, (ii) applies Taylor’s expansion with some
\widetilde{t}
that lies between
t
and
t+uh
, and (iii) utilizes the properties of the second-order kernel function
K
. Similarly, we compute the variance of
\widetilde{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)
as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\left[\widetilde{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nh^{2}}\cdot\mathrm{Var}\left[\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}Y_{i}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nh^{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{K^{2}\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)}{\left[p_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})\right]^{2}}\cdot Y_{i}^{2}\right]-\frac{1}{nh^{2}}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}\cdot Y_{i}\right]\right\}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nh^{2}}\int_{\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{T}}\frac{K^{2}\left(\frac{t_{1}-t}{h}\right)}{\left[p_{T|\bm{S}}(t_{1}|\bm{s}_{1})\right]^{2}}\cdot\left[\mu(t_{1},\bm{s}_{1})^{2}+\sigma^{2}\right]\cdot p(t_{1},\bm{s}_{1})\,dt_{1}d\bm{s}_{1}-\frac{\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S}_{1})\right]\right\}^{2}}{n}+O\left(\frac{h^{2}}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{nh}\int_{\mathcal{S}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{K^{2}(u)}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t+uh|\bm{s}_{1})}\cdot\left[\mu(t+uh,\bm{s}_{1})^{2}+\sigma^{2}\right]p_{S}(\bm{s}_{1})\,dud\bm{s}_{1}+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(ii)}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{nh}\int_{\mathcal{S}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{K^{2}(u)}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})+uh\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t^{\prime}|\bm{s}_{1})}\left[\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})^{2}+2uh\cdot\mu(t^{\prime\prime},\bm{s}_{1})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t^{\prime\prime},\bm{s}_{1})+\sigma^{2}\right]p_{S}(\bm{s}_{1})\,dud\bm{s}_{1}

\displaystyle\quad+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(iii)}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{nh}\int_{\mathcal{S}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{K^{2}(u)}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})}\cdot\left[\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})^{2}+\sigma^{2}\right]p_{S}(\bm{s}_{1})\,dud\bm{s}_{1}+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(iv)}}}{{=}}O\left(\frac{1}{nh}\right),

where (i) uses a change of variable
u=\frac{t_{1}-t}{h}
and the boundedness of
\mu(t,\bm{s})
, (ii) applies the Taylor’s expansion under Assumptions
A3
and
A4
with
t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}
being two points between
t
and
t+uh
, (iii) absorbs the higher order terms to
O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)
, and (iv) utilizes the properties of
K
under Assumption
A5
and the positivity condition (Assumption
A2
). Now, by Chebyshev’s inequality and our above calculations, we obtain that

\displaystyle\widetilde{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)-m(t)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)-\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)\right]-m(t)

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}\left[\widetilde{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)\right]}\right)+O(h^{2})

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{nh}}\right)+O(h^{2})

as
h\to 0
and
nh\to\infty
. As a side note, under the VC-type condition on
K
(Assumption
A5
(c)), we can apply Theorem 2 in
Einmahl and Mason (2005)
to strengthen the above pointwise rate of convergence to the uniform one as:

\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\left|\widetilde{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)-m(t)\right|=O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{|\log h|}{nh}}\right)+O(h^{2}).

E.2.2
Rate of Convergence of
Term V
for
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)

By direct calculations, we have that

Term V

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nh}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}\cdot Y_{i}\left[\frac{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})-\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nh}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}\cdot Y_{i}\left\{\frac{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})-\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})+\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})-\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}{p_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})-\left[p_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})-\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})\right]-\left[\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})-\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})\right]}\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{=}}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]+O(h^{2})+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{nh}}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\times\frac{O_{P}\left(\sup\limits_{|u-t|\leq h}\left|\left|\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})-\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}+\sup\limits_{|u-t|\leq h}\left|\left|\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})-p_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}\right)}{\inf_{(t,\bm{s})\in\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{S}}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s})-O_{P}\left(\sup\limits_{|u-t|\leq h}\left|\left|\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})-\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}+\sup\limits_{|u-t|\leq h}\left|\left|\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})-p_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}\right)}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\Upsilon_{2,n}+\sup\limits_{|u-t|\leq h}\left|\left|\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})-p_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}\right)\left[O(1+h^{2})+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{nh}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\Upsilon_{2,n}+\sup\limits_{|u-t|\leq h}\left|\left|\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})-p_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}\right)

as
h\to 0
and
nh\to\infty
, where (i) utilizes our results for
Term IV
and Markov’s inequality.

Combining our results for
Term IV
and
Term V
in
Section E.2.1
and
Section E.2.2
, we conclude that

\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{IPW}}(t)-m(t)=O(h^{2})+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{nh}}+\Upsilon_{2,n}+\sup\limits_{|u-t|\leq h}\left|\left|\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})-p_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}\right).

E.3
Asymptotic Properties and Double Robustness of
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{DR}}(t)

Finally, we establish the double robustness and asymptotic properties of
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{DR}}(t)
in (
3
). Some parts of the following proof are inspired by the proof of Theorem 3.1 in
Colangelo and Lee (2020)
. Notice that under Assumption
A1
,

\displaystyle\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{DR}}(t)-m(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nh}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\frac{K\left(\frac{T_{i}-t}{h}\right)}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T_{i}|\bm{S}_{i})}\cdot\left[Y_{i}-\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{i})\right]+h\cdot\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{i})\right\}-\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{n}\Psi_{h,t}\left(Y,T,\bm{S};\bar{\mu},\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}\right)-\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]+\mathbb{P}_{n}\left[\Psi_{h,t}\left(Y,T,\bm{S};\widehat{\mu},\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}\right)-\Psi_{h,t}\left(Y,T,\bm{S};\bar{\mu},\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathbb{P}_{n}\Psi_{h,t}\left(Y,T,\bm{S};\bar{\mu},\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}\right)-\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]}_{\textbf{Term VI}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\left(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\right)\left[\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]}_{\textbf{Term VII}}+\underbrace{\left(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\right)\left\{\frac{K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{h}\left[\frac{1}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}-\frac{1}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\right]\left[Y-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]\right\}}_{\textbf{Term VIII}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\left(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\right)\left\{\frac{K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{h\cdot\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\left[\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]\right\}}_{\textbf{Term IX}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\mathbb{P}_{n}\left\{\frac{K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{h}\left[\frac{1}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}-\frac{1}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\right]\left[\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]\right\}}_{\textbf{Term X}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\left\{\left[1-\frac{K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{h\cdot\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\right]\left[\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]\right\}+\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\left\{\frac{K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{h}\left[\frac{1}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}-\frac{1}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\right]\left[Y-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]\right\}}_{\textbf{Term XI}},

where
\Psi_{h,t}\left(Y,T,\bm{S};\mu,p_{T|\bm{S}}\right)=\frac{K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{h\cdot p_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\cdot\left[Y-\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]+\mu(t,\bm{S})
. It remains to show that the dominating
Term VI
is of order
O(h^{2})+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{nh}}\right)
in
Section E.3.1
and the remainder terms are of order
o_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{nh}}\right)
for any
t\in\mathcal{T}
in
Section E.3.2
,
Section E.3.3
,
Section E.3.4
, and
Section E.3.5
. We shall also derive the asymptotic normality of
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{DR}}(t)
in
Section E.3.6
.

E.3.1
Analysis of
Term VI
for
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{DR}}(t)

We analyze the variance and bias of
Term VI
separately as follows. Notice that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\left[\textbf{Term VI}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Var}\left[\mathbb{P}_{n}\Psi_{h,t}\left(Y,T,\bm{S};\bar{\mu},\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nh^{2}}\mathrm{Var}\left[\frac{K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\cdot\left[Y-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]+h\cdot\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{\lesssim}}\frac{1}{nh^{2}}\mathrm{Var}\left[\frac{K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\cdot\left[Y-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]\right]+\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{Var}\left[\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(ii)}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{nh^{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{K^{2}\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(T|\bm{S})}\cdot\left[Y-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]^{2}\right]+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nh^{2}}\int_{\mathcal{S}}\int_{\mathcal{T}}\frac{K^{2}\left(\frac{t_{1}-t}{h}\right)}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(t_{1}|\bm{s}_{1})}\left\{\left[\mu(t_{1},\bm{s}_{1})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{s}_{1})\right]^{2}+\sigma^{2}\right\}p(t_{1},\bm{s}_{1})\,dt_{1}d\bm{s}_{1}+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(iii)}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{nh}\int_{\mathcal{S}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{K^{2}(u)}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(t+uh|\bm{s}_{1})}\left\{\left[\mu(t+uh,\bm{s}_{1})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{s}_{1})\right]^{2}+\sigma^{2}\right\}p(t+uh,\bm{s}_{1})\,dud\bm{s}_{1}+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(iv)}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{nh}\int_{\mathcal{S}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{K^{2}(u)}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(t|\bm{s}_{1})+O(h^{2})}\left\{\left[\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{s}_{1})\right]^{2}+O(h^{2})+\sigma^{2}\right\}\left[p(t,\bm{s}_{1})+O(h)\right]dud\bm{s}_{1}+O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{1}{nh}\right),

where (i) uses Cauchy-Schwarz inequality on the covariance, (ii) uses the boundedness of
\bar{\mu}
under Assumption
A3
to derive the term
O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)
, (iii) leverages a change of variable
u=\frac{t_{1}-t}{h}
, and (iv) applies the Taylor’s expansion under Assumptions
A3
and
A4
. In the above calculations, we also note from the line (i) that the second part
\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})
of
\Psi_{h,t}\left(Y,T,\bm{S};\bar{\mu},\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}\right)
is of smaller order than the first term
\frac{K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{h\cdot\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\left[Y-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]
. Thus, we can only keep the first term in the final asymptotically linear form of
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{DR}}(t)
. Now, by Chebyshev’s inequality, we conclude that

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\right)\Psi_{h,t}\left(Y,T,\bm{S};\bar{\mu},\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}\right)

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}\left[\mathbb{P}_{n}\Psi_{h,t}\left(Y,T,\bm{S};\bar{\mu},\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}\right)\right]}\right)

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{h}}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{n}\psi_{h,t}\left(Y,T,\bm{S};\bar{\mu},\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}\right)\right]}\right)=O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{nh}}\right),

where
\psi_{h,t}\left(Y,T,\bm{S};\bar{\mu},\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}\right)=\frac{K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{\sqrt{h}\cdot\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\left[Y-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]
. In addition, by direct calculations and Taylor’s expansions, we derive that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Bias}\left[\textbf{Term VI}\right]

\displaystyle=\mbox{$\mathrm{P}$}\left[\Psi_{h,t}\left(Y,T,\bm{S};\bar{\mu},\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}\right)\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S}_{1})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{h\cdot\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\left[Y-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{1})-\mu(t,\bm{S}_{1})\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\int_{\mathcal{T}}\frac{K\left(\frac{t_{1}-t}{h}\right)}{h\cdot\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t_{1}|\bm{s}_{1})}\left[\mu(t_{1},\bm{s}_{1})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{s}_{1})\right]p(t_{1},\bm{s}_{1})\,dt_{1}d\bm{s}_{1}+\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{1})-\mu(t,\bm{S}_{1})\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{K(u)}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t+uh|\bm{s}_{1})}\left[\mu(t+uh,\bm{s}_{1})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{s}_{1})\right]p(t+uh,\bm{s}_{1})\,dud\bm{s}_{1}+\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{1})-\mu(t,\bm{S}_{1})\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{K(u)\left[\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{s}_{1})+uh\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})+\frac{u^{2}h^{2}}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})+O(h^{3})\right]}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})+uh\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})+\frac{u^{2}h^{2}}{2}\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})+O(h^{3})}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\left[p(t,\bm{s}_{1})+uh\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p(t,\bm{s}_{1})+\frac{u^{2}h^{2}}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}p(t,\bm{s}_{1})+O(h^{3})\right]\,dud\bm{s}_{1}+\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{1})-\mu(t,\bm{S}_{1})\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}K(u)\left[\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{s}_{1})+uh\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})+\frac{u^{2}h^{2}}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})+O(h^{3})\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\left[\frac{1}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})}-\frac{uh\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(t|\bm{s}_{1})}-\frac{u^{2}h^{2}\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})}{2\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(t|\bm{s}_{1})}+\frac{u^{2}h^{2}\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})\right]^{2}}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{3}(t|\bm{s}_{1})}+O(h^{3})\right]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\left[p(t,\bm{s}_{1})+uh\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p(t,\bm{s}_{1})+\frac{u^{2}h^{2}}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}p(t,\bm{s}_{1})+O(h^{3})\right]\,dud\bm{s}_{1}+\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S}_{1})-\mu(t,\bm{S}_{1})\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\frac{\left[\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{s}_{1})\right]}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})}\cdot p(t,\bm{s}_{1})\,d\bm{s}_{1}+\int_{\mathcal{S}}\left[\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{s}_{1})-\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})\right]p_{S}(\bm{s}_{1})\,d\bm{s}_{1}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{h^{2}\kappa_{2}}{2}\int_{\mathcal{S}}\Bigg\{\frac{\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})}\cdot p(t,\bm{s}_{1})+\frac{2\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})\right]^{2}/\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(t|\bm{s}_{1})}\left[\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{s}_{1})-\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})\right]p(t,\bm{s}_{1})

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{\left[\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{s}_{1})-\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})\right]}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})}\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}p(t,\bm{s}_{1})-\frac{2\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})\right]\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})\right]}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(t|\bm{s}_{1})}\cdot p(t,\bm{s}_{1})+\frac{2\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})\right]\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p(t,\bm{s}_{1})\right]}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})}

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\frac{2\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p(t,\bm{s}_{1})\right]\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})\right]\left[\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{s}_{1})-\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})\right]}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(t|\bm{s}_{1})}\Bigg\}d\bm{s}_{1}+o(h^{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\frac{\left[\mu(t,\bm{s}_{1})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{s}_{1})\right]\left[p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})-\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})\right]}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})\cdot p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s}_{1})}\cdot p(t,\bm{s}_{1})\,d\bm{s}_{1}+h^{2}B_{m}(t)+o(h^{2})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{S}}\left\{\frac{\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]\left[p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})-\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\right]}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}\right\}+h^{2}B_{m}(t)+o(h^{2}),

where the complicated bias term
B_{m}(t)
is given by

\displaystyle B_{m}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{2}}{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\bm{S}}\Bigg\{\frac{\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}\Bigg[\frac{2\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}\cdot p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad-\frac{2\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\right]\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\right]}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}\Bigg]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\bm{S}}\left\{\frac{\left[\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mu(t,\bm{S})-2\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\right]\right]}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(t|\bm{S})}\cdot p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\right\}

\displaystyle\quad+\kappa_{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\bm{S}}\left[\frac{\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}\right]

and
\bar{p}(t,\bm{s})=\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{s})\cdot p_{S}(\bm{s})
. Under the condition that either
\bar{\mu}=\mu
or
\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}=p_{T|\bm{S}}
, we have that

\mathbb{E}_{\bm{S}}\left\{\frac{\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]\left[p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})-\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\right]}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}\right\}=0

and

\displaystyle B_{m}(t)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{\kappa_{2}}{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\bm{S}}\left\{\frac{\left[\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mu(t,\bm{S})-2\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\right]\right]}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(t|\bm{S})}\cdot p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})+\frac{2\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}\right\}\,\text{ when }\bar{\mu}=\mu,\\
\frac{\kappa_{2}}{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\bm{S}}\left[\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]\quad\quad\text{ when }\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}=p_{T|\bm{S}},\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{\kappa_{2}}{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\bm{S}}\left\{\frac{2\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})+p_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\left[\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mu(t,\bm{S})-2\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})}\right\}\quad\text{ when }\bar{\mu}=\mu,\\
\frac{\kappa_{2}}{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\bm{S}}\left[\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]\quad\quad\text{ when }\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}=p_{T|\bm{S}}.\end{cases}

As a result, as
h\to 0
and
nh\to\infty
, we have that

Term VI

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{n}\Psi_{h,t}\left(Y,T,\bm{S};\bar{\mu},\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}\right)-\mathbb{E}\left[\mu(t,\bm{S})\right]

\displaystyle=h^{2}B_{m}(t)+o(h^{2})+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{nh}}\right)

\displaystyle=O(h^{2})+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{nh}}\right).

As a side note, under some VC-type condition on the kernel function
K

(
Einmahl and Mason, 2005
)
, we can strengthen the above pointwise rate of convergence to the following uniform one as:

\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\left|\textbf{Term VI}\right|=O(h^{2})+O_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{|\log h|}{nh}}\right);

see Theorem 4 in
Einmahl and Mason (2005)
for details.

E.3.2
Analysis of
Term VII
for
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{DR}}(t)

By Markov’s inequality, we know that

\displaystyle\sqrt{nh}\cdot\textbf{Term VII}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{h}\cdot\mathbb{G}_{n}\left[\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\sqrt{h}\cdot\Upsilon_{1,n}\right)=o_{P}(1)

because
\mathbb{E}\left\{h\cdot\left[\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]^{2}\right\}=h\cdot\left|\left|\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}^{2}=O_{P}\left(h\cdot\Upsilon_{1,n}^{2}\right)
and
\Upsilon_{1,n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. As a side note, under Assumption
A3
on
\bar{\mu}
and
\widehat{\mu}
, we know that the function
\bm{s}\mapsto\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{s})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{s})
is Lipschitz continuous with respect to
t\in\mathcal{T}
. Together with the compactness of
\mathcal{T}
and Example 19.7 in
van der Vaart (1998)
, we can also deduce that

\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\left|\sqrt{h}\cdot\mathbb{G}_{n}\left[\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]\right|=O_{P}\left(\sqrt{h}\cdot\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\left|\left|\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}\right),

which will be
o_{P}(1)
as well if
\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\left|\left|\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}=o_{P}(1)
.

E.3.3
Analyses of
Term VIII
and
Term IX
for
\widehat{m}_{\mathrm{DR}}(t)

The argument for showing
Term VIII
and
Term IX
to be
o_{P}\left(\sqrt{\frac{1}{nh}}\right)
will be similar to the one for
Term VII
above. By Markov’s inequality, we know that

\displaystyle\sqrt{nh}\cdot\textbf{Term VIII}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{G}_{n}\left[\frac{K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{\sqrt{h}}\left[\frac{1}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}-\frac{1}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\right]\left[Y-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]\right]

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\Upsilon_{2,n}\right)=o_{P}(1)

because

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\{\frac{K^{2}\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{h}\cdot\frac{\left[\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})-\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})\right]^{2}}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(T|\bm{S})\cdot\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(T|\bm{S})}\cdot\left[Y-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\{\frac{K^{2}\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{h}\cdot\frac{\left[\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})-\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})\right]^{2}}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(T|\bm{S})\cdot\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(T|\bm{S})}\cdot\left[\left(\mu(T,\bm{S})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right)^{2}+\sigma^{2}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}K^{2}(u)\cdot\frac{\left[\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t+uh|\bm{S})-\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t+uh|\bm{S})\right]^{2}p_{T|\bm{S}}(t+uh|\bm{S})}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(T|\bm{S})\cdot\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(T|\bm{S})}\cdot\left[\left(\mu(t+uh,\bm{S})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right)^{2}+\sigma^{2}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(ii)}}}{{\lesssim}}\sup_{|u-t|\leq h}\left|\left|\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})-\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(iii)}}}{{=}}O_{P}\left(\Upsilon_{2,n}^{2}\right)=o_{P}(1),

where (i) uses the change of variable
u=\frac{T-t}{h}
in the integration, (ii) leverages the boundedness of
\mu,\bar{\mu}
under Assumption
A3
and the positivity condition (Assumption
A2
) on
\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}
, as well as (iii) applies
\sup\limits_{|u-t|\leq h}\left|\left|\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})-\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(u|\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}=O_{P}\left(\Upsilon_{2,n}\right)
with
\Upsilon_{2,n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. As a side note again, under the VC-type condition on the kernel function
K

(
Einmahl and Mason, 2005
)
and
\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\left|\left|\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})-\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(t|\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}=O_{P}\left(\Upsilon_{2,n}\right)=o_{P}(1)
, we can strengthen the above pointwise rate of convergence to the following uniform result as:

\sup_{t\in\mathcal{T}}\left|\mathbb{G}_{n}\left[\frac{K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{\sqrt{h}}\left[\frac{1}{\widehat{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}-\frac{1}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\right]\left[Y-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]\right]\right|=o_{P}(1).

Similarly, by Markov’s inequality, we have that

\displaystyle\sqrt{nh}\cdot\textbf{Term IX}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{G}_{n}\left\{\frac{K\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{\sqrt{h}\cdot\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}(T|\bm{S})}\left[\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]\right\}=O_{P}\left(\Upsilon_{1,n}\right)=o_{P}(1)

because

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\{\frac{K^{2}\left(\frac{T-t}{h}\right)}{h\cdot\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(T|\bm{S})}\left[\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\{\int_{\mathcal{T}}\frac{K^{2}\left(\frac{t_{1}-t}{h}\right)\cdot p_{T|\bm{S}}(t_{1}|\bm{S})}{h\cdot\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(t_{1}|\bm{S})}\left[\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]^{2}dt_{1}\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{K^{2}(u)\cdot p_{T|\bm{S}}(t+uh|\bm{S})}{\bar{p}_{T|\bm{S}}^{2}(t+uh|\bm{S})}\left[\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right]^{2}du\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(ii)}}}{{\lesssim}}\left|\left|\widehat{\mu}(t,\bm{S})-\bar{\mu}(t,\bm{S})\right|\right|_{L_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\Upsilon_{1,n}^{2}\right)=o_{P}(1),
